Throttling ball streaming by measured UI frame times

The core regulates ball spawn rates using front-end frame times to stay within a 16.67 ms rendering budget.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/screens/GameplayScreen.cpp, source/core/game/AdditionGovernor.cpp, source/core/game/SessionRunner.h

source/frontends/sdl/screens/GameplayScreen.cpp lines 220-232
// The physics thread keeps its own pace; this hands it the pause state, the UI's frame timings for the addition governor
// and the visibility slot size. The view drawn this frame was taken above, so commands posted while drawing show
// from a later view.
// Commit 1ae4a07ae: throttles ball addition dynamically to prevent frame drops during massive spawns.
void GameplayScreen::tickPhysics(const GameplayLayout& layout) {
    const FrameSample& lastFrame = app.frameTimings().latest();
    PhysicsFrameInput input;
    input.paused = app.flow().isPaused();
    // AdditionGovernor scales spawns back if max(cpuMs, gpuMs) exceeds the 16.67 ms budget.
    input.uiWorkSeconds = std::max(lastFrame.cpuMs, lastFrame.gpuMs) / millisecondsPerSecond;
    input.visibilitySlotCells = BallView::slotCells(layout.board, view->ballRadius);
    input.drawing = app.settings().drawBoard;
    input.framesPerSecond = app.settings().physicsFramesPerSecond;
    runner->tick(input);
}

High-capacity ball rendering and visibility compute

Packing a ball's position and colour into one 32-bit word

Packing 2D position and color into a 32-bit word cuts PCIe bandwidth by 75% with sub-pixel precision.

Source: source/core/sim/kernels/BallStepCore.h, source/frontends/sdl/shaders/Balls.hlsl, source/frontends/unreal/Shaders/BallsDraw.usf

source/core/sim/kernels/BallStepCore.h lines 60-63
// 4-byte pack cuts PCIe bandwidth by 75% vs float4; 0xFFFFFFFFu represents an empty visibility slot.
BALLS_HOST_DEVICE unsigned int packBallForRender(float x, float y, int color, float scaleX, float scaleY) {
    // 14 bits each for X and Y yield 16,384 steps (~0.11 px on 1080p), eliminating stepping artifacts.
    return packRenderCoordinate(x, scaleX) | (packRenderCoordinate(y, scaleY) << renderCoordinateBits) |
           // Bits 28-30 store the 3-bit color palette index (colors 0-6).
           (static_cast<unsigned int>(color) << renderColorShift);
}

The four-pass GPU visibility pipeline

A four-stage GPU visibility pipeline resolves screen tile occupancy, rasterizing only winning balls.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/shaders/BallVisibility.hlsl, source/frontends/sdl/render/nvrhi/NvrhiRenderer.cpp

source/frontends/sdl/shaders/BallVisibility.hlsl lines 55-68
// Four passes resolve visibility: Clear (reset), Bid (InterlockedMax), Resolve, and Compact (prefix sum).
[numthreads(256, 1, 1)]
void computeMain(uint3 thread : SV_DispatchThreadID) {
    if (thread.x >= itemCount) {
        return;
    }
    const uint priority = slotPriority(thread.x);
    // Subsamples bidders via mask to reduce atomic contention above tens of millions of balls.
    if (((priority >> 1) & bidderMask) != 0u) {
        return;
    }
    const uint slot = slotForPacked(balls[thread.x]);
    // Early check skips atomic instruction if another thread already holds higher priority.
    if (priority > priorities[slot]) {
        InterlockedMax(priorities[slot], priority);
    }
}

Halving the visibility grid slot: measured visual and performance impact

Sizing visibility grid slots to 1.0 ball radius eliminates background gaps while avoiding per-pixel overhead.

Source: Commit c4e70dae9, source/frontends/sdl/render/nvrhi/NvrhiRenderer.cpp, source/frontends/sdl/render/BallView.cpp

source/core/sim/PhysicsBackend.h lines 112-126
// The visibility grid has one slot per ball radius: each slot draws the one ball that won it, where that ball is.
// At 1.6 radii a sixth of a full board showed through, since two neighbouring winners can sit 3.2 radii apart while a
// ball covers 2; at one radius under 0.2% does (about 4% at 1.3). The grid has 2.6 times the slots of 1.6: on a GTX
// 1650 a full DA_1000Bricks board went from 0.21 to 0.33 ms of GPU time a frame and still filled to 2.9 billion balls.
// Commit c4e70dae9: 1.0 radius has 2.6x more slots than 1.6, adding only 0.34 ms on GTX 1650.
// Cuts background show-through from 16.6% to 0.1%; 1 slot/pixel was rejected for adding ~5 ms.
inline constexpr float visibilitySlotsPerRadius = 1.0f;

// GPU backends record each step's kills in a buffer sized for the most a step can make. Bricks with huge hit counts
// would ask for gigabytes, so the buffer stops here; a kill past it is refunded and the ball bounces, to land a later
// step.
// Caps kill buffer at 4M entries to prevent multi-GB allocations; excess kills bounce and retry.
inline constexpr size_t maximumKillsPerStep = size_t{1} << 22;

// A slot is never smaller than a pixel: a board bigger than the screen draws at most one ball per pixel.
// Clamps slots to >= 1 px and radius to >= 0.75 px to cover pixel centers without sub-pixel overhead.
inline float visibilitySlotSize(float radiusCells, float cellPixels) {
    return std::max(radiusCells * visibilitySlotsPerRadius, cellPixels > 0.0f ? 1.0f / cellPixels : 0.0f);
}

Indirect draws without CPU readback

GPU compute passes write indirect draw arguments directly, eliminating CPU readbacks and PCIe stalls.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/shaders/BallVisibility.hlsl, source/frontends/sdl/shaders/BallVisibility.hlsl, source/frontends/sdl/render/nvrhi/NvrhiRenderer.cpp

source/frontends/sdl/shaders/BallVisibility.hlsl lines 89-106
// Layout matches VkDrawIndirectCommand directly, avoiding CPU readback stalls and PCIe round trips.
#if defined(COMPACT_PASS)
StructuredBuffer<uint> candidates : register(t0, space0);
RWStructuredBuffer<uint> visibleBalls : register(u0, space1);
// Bound to indirectParams for commandList->drawIndirect(0).
RWStructuredBuffer<uint> drawArguments : register(u1, space1);

[numthreads(256, 1, 1)]
void computeMain(uint3 thread : SV_DispatchThreadID) {
    if (thread.x >= itemCount) {
        return;
    }
    const uint packedBall = candidates[thread.x];
    if (packedBall == emptySlot) {
        return;
    }
    uint destination;
    // SDL draws 6-vertex quads while Unreal draws 3-vertex equilateral triangles via QuadCorners[3].
    InterlockedAdd(drawArguments[1], 1u, destination);
    visibleBalls[destination] = packedBall;
}

Rendering dense boards with sub-4-pixel cells, and the measured speedups

Under 4 px per cell, SDL and Unreal replace per-brick geometry with a single tiled fullscreen quad shader.

Source: Commits 981f7e64d and 0c9d30943, source/frontends/sdl/shaders/CellImage.hlsl, source/frontends/unreal/Shaders/BallsCells.usf

source/frontends/sdl/shaders/CellImage.hlsl lines 40-55
// Discrete quads stalled: Unreal took 175 ms GPU; SDL spent 79 ms CPU on 4M bricks and 289 ms on 16.8M.
// Single quad shader: commit 981f7e64d (SDL), commit 0c9d30943 (Unreal); 8192x8192 (67M) hits 177 FPS.
float4 fragmentMain(VertexOutput input) : SV_Target0 {
    // Maps screen pixels to cells; labels under 4 px are skipped, saving up to 250 ms CPU per frame.
    const float2 cell = floor((input.pixel - boardOrigin) / cellPixels);
    if (cell.x < 0.0 || cell.y < 0.0 || cell.x >= columns || cell.y >= rows) {
        discard;
    }
    const uint column = (uint)cell.x;
    const uint row = (uint)cell.y;
    // Tiled layout keeps spatial lookups cache-friendly.
    const uint tile = (row / tileSize) * tilesAcross + column / tileSize;
    const uint index = tile * cellsPerTile + (row % tileSize) * tileSize + column % tileSize;
    const uint shade = (shadeWords[index >> 2] >> ((index & 3) * 8)) & 0xFF;
    if (shade == 0) {
        discard;
    }
    const uint color = (shade & ~flashBit) - 1;
    return palette[(shade & flashBit) != 0 ? color + colorCount : color];
}

SDL renderer abstraction and NVRHI

The IRenderer abstraction in the SDL front end

IRenderer provides a tailored interface for UI cards, brick grids, and balls across backend APIs.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/render/IRenderer.h

source/frontends/sdl/render/IRenderer.h lines 69-98
// Implemented by NvrhiRenderer (Vulkan, D3D12), WebGpuRenderer (wgpu, browser), and SdlGpuRenderer.
class IRenderer {
public:
    virtual ~IRenderer() = default;

    virtual bool beginFrame() = 0;
    virtual void drawRects(std::span<const RendererRect> rects) = 0;
    virtual void drawBalls(const BallDrawRequest& request) = 0;
    // Single-pass quad shader for dense brick layouts when cell size is under 4 pixels.
    // False when this renderer has no cell image pass; the caller then draws the bricks as rectangles.
    virtual bool drawCellImage(const CellImageDrawRequest&) { return false; }
    virtual void endFrame(ClearColor clearColor) = 0;

    virtual float viewWidth() const = 0;
    virtual float viewHeight() const = 0;
    virtual bool setVsync(bool enabled) = 0;
    virtual bool isVsyncEnabled() const = 0;
    virtual void requestScreenshot(const std::string& path) = 0;
    virtual bool lastScreenshotSucceeded() const = 0;
    virtual double lastGpuWaitMs() const = 0;
    virtual double lastGpuBusyMs() const = 0;
    // Whether the last frame reached the screen; a frame the compositor was not ready for is rendered but not shown.
    virtual bool lastFramePresented() const { return true; }
    // Time the last frame spent waiting for the display (VSync, a busy compositor), not doing work.
    virtual double lastPresentWaitMs() const { return 0.0; }
    virtual std::string deviceDescription() const = 0;
    // Allows off-thread initialization of devices, pipelines, and buffers before taking over the window.
    // A renderer that supports it is prepared off the UI thread (device, pipelines, buffers) and only then takes
    // over the window, so switching renderer or GPU costs the frame no more than the swap-chain hand-over.
    virtual bool supportsDeferredAttach() const { return false; }
    virtual bool attachToWindow(std::string&) { return true; }
    virtual void detachFromWindow() {}
};

NVRHI as the desktop renderer, and Vulkan as the default on Windows

NVRHI abstracts Vulkan and D3D12, defaulting to Vulkan so rendering and physics share one driver stack.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/render/RendererFactory.cpp, data/docs/codebase.html

source/frontends/sdl/render/RendererFactory.cpp lines 22-31
// Windows use SDL_WINDOW_VULKAN to support both Vulkan surfaces and runtime D3D12 switching.
GraphicsApi graphicsApiFor(std::string_view requested) {
    // Sharing Vulkan across renderer and physics avoids dual runtimes; D3D12 selectable via --api d3d12.
    // Vulkan is the default everywhere: the physics compute plugin is Vulkan-only, so the renderer sharing its API
    // keeps one driver stack per GPU. Direct3D 12 stays available on Windows when asked for.
#if defined(_WIN32)
    return requested == "d3d12" ? GraphicsApi::D3d12 : GraphicsApi::Vulkan;
#else
    (void)requested;
    return GraphicsApi::Vulkan;
#endif
}

Off-thread renderer preparation against GPU switch hitches

Renderer initialization runs on a worker thread to eliminate multi-hundred-millisecond hitches during GPU switches.

Source: Commit 3b523ecff, source/frontends/sdl/render/nvrhi/NvrhiRenderer.cpp, source/frontends/sdl/app/App.cpp

source/frontends/sdl/app/App.cpp lines 235-265
// Commit 3b523ecff: off-thread prepareRenderer eliminated 180 ms device setup and 345-660 ms hitches.
// WebGPU and SDL_GPU require window surfaces at creation, so only deferred-attach renderers use this path.
// Opening a device and building its pipelines takes up to a few hundred milliseconds, so a renderer that can be
// prepared apart from the window is built on a worker thread while the current one keeps drawing; the frame only
// pays for the swap-chain hand-over in adoptPreparedRenderer. Other renderers still switch on the frame.
void App::switchRenderer(RendererTarget target) {
    if (!isRendererCompiledIn(target.renderer)) {
        // Tearing the working renderer down only to find the wanted one missing would cost a rebuild for nothing.
        SDL_Log("Renderer %s is not in this build", target.renderer.c_str());
        showToast(std::format("This build has no {} renderer", target.renderer), true);
        return;
    }
    // Retires any in-flight preparation task on a separate thread to avoid blocking the main thread.
    if (preparing.valid()) {
        // The future of a std::async waits in its destructor, so an abandoned build finishes on its own thread.
        std::thread([abandoned = std::move(preparing)]() mutable { retireRenderer(abandoned.get().renderer); }).detach();
    }
    preparingTarget.reset();
    if (!renderer || !renderer->supportsDeferredAttach()) {
        switchRendererNow(target);
        return;
    }
    preparingTarget = target;
    SDL_Window* targetWindow = window;
    preparing = std::async(std::launch::async, [targetWindow, target] {
        PreparedRenderer prepared;
        prepared.renderer =
            prepareRenderer(targetWindow, target.renderer, target.api, target.gpuPreference, prepared.error);
        if (prepared.renderer) {
            prepared.adapters = enumerateGpuAdapters(adapterApiFor(target.renderer, target.api));
        }
        return prepared;
    });
}

The backend-switch crash and upload staging

Staging ball data into upload memory during draw calls prevents use-after-free crashes when backends switch.

Source: Commit 684860db8, source/frontends/sdl/render/nvrhi/NvrhiRenderer.cpp

source/frontends/sdl/render/nvrhi/NvrhiRenderer.cpp lines 474-488
// Commit 684860db8: fixed crash where /fill or backend switch reallocated 10M balls before endFrame.
// NVRHI writes directly to mapped PCIe staging; WebGPU and SDL_GPU stage into std::vector<uint32_t>.
// The ball words live in the physics backend, which a control request or a UI action later in the same frame may
// reallocate or free, so they are copied out here: writeBuffer copies into upload memory when it is called.
void NvrhiRenderer::drawBalls(const BallDrawRequest& request) {
    pendingBalls = request;
    hasBalls = request.count > 0 && request.packed;
    if (!hasBalls) {
        return;
    }
    ensureBuffer(ballBuffer, ballCapacity, request.count * storageWord, storageWord, false, false, "balls");
    ballUploadList->open();
    // Writes to staging memory immediately before simulation pointers can invalidate.
    ballUploadList->writeBuffer(ballBuffer, request.packed, request.count * storageWord);
    ballUploadList->close();
    pendingBalls.packed = nullptr;
    ballsUploaded = true;
}

    # The assets are packed into the .wasm's own file system, the physics backend is linked in, and the browser

The single-threaded browser build and its tick

The web build runs single-threaded without pthreads, stepping physics synchronously on the UI frame.

Source: scripts/web/README.md, source/frontends/sdl/screens/GameplayScreen.cpp

scripts/web/README.md lines 38-47
# Static hosts lack COOP/COEP for SharedArrayBuffer, so pthreads are disabled.
# Physics ticks synchronously on UI thread, handling over 100k balls in Scalar backend.
## What the browser build leaves out

- **Threads.** The browser has no pthreads here (that would need `SharedArrayBuffer` and cross-origin isolation
  headers a static host does not send), so `SessionRunner` runs a physics frame from the UI thread in `tick()`
  instead of on a thread of its own. Physics therefore advances once per drawn frame.
- **Physics plugins.** There are no loadable plugin files, so the scalar backend is linked into the executable and
  `BackendFactoryWeb.cpp` stands in for the plugin-aware factory; every other backend reports that it is not
  available.
- **The control API.** Sockets do not exist in the page, so `ControlServerWebStub.cpp` replaces the control server.
- **NVRHI, CUDA, ROCm and the Vulkan plugin**, along with the native tools, are not built for this target.

Asynchronous WebGPU adapter and device setup without blocking

WebGPU adapter acquisition uses ASYNCIFY to yield C++ execution to the browser event loop without freezing.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/render/webgpu/WebGpuRenderer.cpp, source/frontends/sdl/render/webgpu/WebGpuRenderer.cpp, source/frontends/sdl/CMakeLists.txt

source/frontends/sdl/render/webgpu/WebGpuRenderer.cpp lines 1775-1800
// WebGPU adapter creation is an async JS promise; polling would freeze the browser page.
// Emscripten -sASYNCIFY pauses C++ execution; browser automatically presents swap chain after turn.
WGPURequestAdapterCallbackInfo adapterCallbackInfo{};
adapterCallbackInfo.mode = WGPUCallbackMode_AllowSpontaneous;
adapterCallbackInfo.callback = [](WGPURequestAdapterStatus status, WGPUAdapter adapter, WGPUStringView message,
                                  void* u1, void*) {
    auto* pl = static_cast<AdapterPayload*>(u1);
    pl->complete = true;
    if (status == WGPURequestAdapterStatus_Success) {
        pl->adapter = adapter;
    } else {
        pl->error = std::string(fromStringView(message));
    }
};
adapterCallbackInfo.userdata1 = &adapterPayload;
WGPURequestAdapterOptions adapterOptions{};
adapterOptions.compatibleSurface = surface;
wgpuInstanceRequestAdapter(instance, &adapterOptions, adapterCallbackInfo);
// emscripten_sleep unwinds C++ stack via ASYNCIFY, yielding control until callback fires.
while (!adapterPayload.complete) {
    emscripten_sleep(16);
}
if (!adapterPayload.adapter) {
    error = adapterPayload.error.empty() ? std::string("wgpuInstanceRequestAdapter failed")
                                        : adapterPayload.error;
    wgpuSurfaceRelease(surface);
    wgpuInstanceRelease(instance);
    return nullptr;
}

Headless web automation without TCP sockets

A JavaScript bridge exports ballsControl for headless web automation without TCP sockets.

Source: source/frontends/sdl/control/ControlBridgeWeb.cpp, scripts/web/README.md

source/frontends/sdl/control/ControlBridgeWeb.cpp lines 14-32
// ControlServerWebStub replaces HTTP server; URL query params (?cmd=/play) run routes headlessly.
// Commit 2fd7d321a: exported _main and _ballsControl to prevent dead-code linker pruning.
void setWebControlBridge(std::function<std::optional<ControlResponse>(const ControlRequest&)> newHandler) {
    handler = std::move(newHandler);
    SDL_Log("[control] web bridge registered");
    // Sets window.ballsReady so browser automation knows when commands can run.
    // The page can tell when the game is ready to take commands, instead of guessing a delay.
    EM_ASM(window.ballsReady = true);
}

// The page's entry point: the request target of the desktop API ("/play?mode=performance&level=DA_1000Bricks") in,
// that route's JSON out. The result stays valid until the next call, which is all a JavaScript string decode needs.
extern "C" EMSCRIPTEN_KEEPALIVE const char* ballsControl(const char* target) {
    static std::string response;
    if (!handler) {
        response = R"({"ok":false,"error":"the game is still loading"})";
        return response.c_str();
    }
    const std::optional<ControlResponse> answer = handler(parseControlTarget(target));
    // Retains response in static string so pointer stays valid across JS FFI boundary.
    response = answer ? answer->json : std::string(R"({"ok":false,"error":"unknown route"})");
    return response.c_str();
}

WebGPU buffer limits and measured WebAssembly performance

WebGPU shaders stay within browser limits, simulating over one hundred thousand balls at high framerates.

Source: scripts/web/README.md, source/frontends/sdl/render/webgpu/WebGpuRenderer.cpp

scripts/web/README.md lines 72-78
# # Chrome 156 benchmark: Edge_Cage reached 123k balls at ~200 FPS in debug WebAssembly.
- `BALLS_RENDERER=webgpu` is the default for Emscripten; WebGPU comes from Emscripten's `emdawnwebgpu` port rather
  than from wgpu-native.
- wgpu-native-only code (adapter enumeration, `wgpuSurfacePresent`) is guarded by `#if !defined(__EMSCRIPTEN__)`.
  The browser asks for the default adapter of the canvas surface and lets `wgpuInstanceProcessEvents` present.
- Adapter and device requests are asynchronous, so the renderer waits for them with `emscripten_sleep` (ASYNCIFY).
# # Visibility compute passes use at most 3 storage buffers, well within browser limit of 8.
- The visibility compute passes use at most three storage buffers per stage, which fits the browser's default
  `maxStorageBuffersPerShaderStage` of 8.

Unreal Engine 5 integration and high-end renderer showcases

The Unreal front end's three modules and the external BallsCore module

Unreal splits into three modules; CMake builds BallsCore externally to keep compiler flag parity.

Source: source/frontends/unreal/Source/BallsCore/BallsCore.Build.cs, source/frontends/unreal/Source/BallsRendering/BallsRendering.Build.cs, data/docs/codebase.html

source/frontends/unreal/Source/BallsCore/BallsCore.Build.cs lines 7-50
// BallsCore is built with CMake rather than UBT to maintain compiler flag and ABI parity.
// BuildCore.bat uses balls_build_core.lock to serialize CMake builds during parallel UBT runs.
public BallsCore(ReadOnlyTargetRules Target) : base(Target)
{
    Type = ModuleType.External;

    string RepoRoot = Path.GetFullPath(Path.Combine(ModuleDirectory, "..", "..", "..", "..", ".."));
    PublicSystemIncludePaths.Add(Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "source", "core"));
    if (Target.Platform == UnrealTargetPlatform.Android)
    {
        PublicAdditionalLibraries.Add(Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "android-ue", "source", "core", "libballs_core.a"));
    }
    // Linux bundles libballs_physics_cpu.so and libballs_physics_vulkan.so next to the binary.
    else if (Target.Platform == UnrealTargetPlatform.Linux)
    {
        // Cross-compiled with Unreal's own Linux toolchain by BuildCoreLinux.bat, so core, plugins and the game
        // share one libc++. The physics plugins are .so files the core dlopens from next to the executable.
        PublicAdditionalLibraries.Add(Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "linux-ue", "source", "core", "libballs_core.a"));
        string PluginDirectory = Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "linux-ue", "bin");
        PublicDefinitions.Add("BALLS_PLUGIN_DIR=\"" + PluginDirectory.Replace('\\', '/') + "\"");
        foreach (string Plugin in new[] { "libballs_physics_cpu.so", "libballs_physics_vulkan.so" })
        {
            string PluginPath = Path.Combine(PluginDirectory, Plugin);
            if (File.Exists(PluginPath))
            {
                RuntimeDependencies.Add("$(BinaryOutputDir)/" + Plugin, PluginPath);
            }
        }
    }
    // Windows bundles balls_physics_cpu.dll and balls_physics_cuda.dll next to binaries.
    else
    {
        PublicAdditionalLibraries.Add(Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "win32-ue", "source", "core", "Release", "balls_core.lib"));
        // The physics backends (CPU, CUDA) are DLLs the core loads at run time. Packaged builds stage them next
        // to the executable; the editor finds them through BALLS_PLUGIN_DIR (see FBallsApp).
        string PluginDirectory = Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "win32-ue", "bin", "Release");
        PublicDefinitions.Add("BALLS_PLUGIN_DIR=\"" + PluginDirectory.Replace('\\', '/') + "\"");
        foreach (string Plugin in new[] { "balls_physics_cpu.dll", "balls_physics_cuda.dll" })
        {
            string PluginPath = Path.Combine(PluginDirectory, Plugin);
            if (File.Exists(PluginPath))
            {
                RuntimeDependencies.Add("$(BinaryOutputDir)/" + Plugin, PluginPath);
            }
        }
    }
    PublicDefinitions.Add("BALLS_ASSET_DIR=\"" + Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "data", "assets").Replace('\\', '/') + "\"");
}

Deploying and discovering physics plugins in Unreal builds

Physics backends deploy as dynamic libraries alongside Unreal binaries, discovered via runtime paths.

Source: source/frontends/unreal/Source/BallsCore/BallsCore.Build.cs, source/frontends/unreal/Source/Balls/Private/BallsApp.cpp

source/frontends/unreal/Source/BallsCore/BallsCore.Build.cs lines 34-48
// Standalone plugin DLLs/so load dynamically; Linux builds use Unreal's Clang and libc++ for ABI match.
{
    PublicAdditionalLibraries.Add(Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "win32-ue", "source", "core", "Release", "balls_core.lib"));
    // Editor points BALLS_PLUGIN_DIR to CMake bin; packaged builds look in GetModulesDirectory().
    // The physics backends (CPU, CUDA) are DLLs the core loads at run time. Packaged builds stage them next
    // to the executable; the editor finds them through BALLS_PLUGIN_DIR (see FBallsApp).
    string PluginDirectory = Path.Combine(RepoRoot, "build", "win32-ue", "bin", "Release");
    PublicDefinitions.Add("BALLS_PLUGIN_DIR=\"" + PluginDirectory.Replace('\\', '/') + "\"");
    foreach (string Plugin in new[] { "balls_physics_cpu.dll", "balls_physics_cuda.dll" })
    {
        string PluginPath = Path.Combine(PluginDirectory, Plugin);
        if (File.Exists(PluginPath))
        {
            // Stages plugin binaries into $(BinaryOutputDir) alongside cooked game binary.
            RuntimeDependencies.Add("$(BinaryOutputDir)/" + Plugin, PluginPath);
        }
    }
}

Rendering balls through a custom RDG scene view extension

RDG scene view extension renders ball impostors after motion blur to avoid ghosting without velocity vectors.

Source: source/frontends/unreal/Source/BallsRendering/Private/BallsSceneViewExtension.cpp, source/frontends/unreal/Shaders/BallsDraw.usf

source/frontends/unreal/Shaders/BallsDraw.usf lines 20-32
// Hooking after MotionBlur prevents ghosting (impostors lack velocity vectors) while keeping bloom.
// Draws circumscribing equilateral triangles (3 vertices) per ball via DrawPrimitive.
void MainVS(uint VertexId : SV_VertexID, uint InstanceId : SV_InstanceID,
            out float4 OutPosition : SV_POSITION, out float2 OutLocal : TEXCOORD0,
            out nointerpolation float3 OutColor : TEXCOORD1)
{
    const uint Packed = PackedBalls[InstanceId];
    const float2 Center = float2(Packed & 16383u, (Packed >> 14u) & 16383u) * UnpackScale;
    const float2 Local = QuadCorners[VertexId];
    OutPosition = mul(float4(Center + Local * Radius, 0.0, 1.0), BoardToClip);
    // Discards empty visibility slots (0xFFFFFFFF) by projecting them beyond clip planes.
    // 0xFFFFFFFF marks a visibility-grid slot no ball won; it must not decode into a ball in the corner.
    OutPosition = (VertexOnly > 0.5 || Packed == 0xFFFFFFFFu) ? float4(1.0e6, 1.0e6, 0.0, 1.0) : OutPosition;
    // Pixel shader discards fragments where dot(Local, Local) > 1.0.
    OutLocal = Local;
    OutColor = Palette[(Packed >> 28u) & 7u].rgb;
}

source/frontends/unreal/Source/Balls/Private/BallsGraphics.cpp lines 378-402
// Lumen and TSR affect background and bricks; post-process ball impostors bypass them.
const bool bLumen = UnavailableLumen().IsEmpty();
Run(FString::Printf(TEXT("r.DynamicGlobalIlluminationMethod %d"),
                    bLumen && Settings.GlobalIllumination == EBallsGlobalIllumination::Lumen ? 1 : 0));
const int32 ReflectionMethod = Settings.Reflections == EBallsReflections::Lumen ? (bLumen ? 1 : 2)
                               : Settings.Reflections == EBallsReflections::ScreenSpace ? 2
                                                                                        : 0;
Run(FString::Printf(TEXT("r.ReflectionMethod %d"), ReflectionMethod));
Run(FString::Printf(TEXT("r.Shadow.Virtual.Enable %d"),
                    Settings.Shadows == EBallsShadows::Virtual && UnavailableVirtualShadows().IsEmpty() ? 1 : 0));
Run(FString::Printf(TEXT("r.Nanite %d"), Settings.bNanite && UnavailableNanite().IsEmpty() ? 1 : 0));

IConsoleVariable* Fsr = InstalledFsrCvar();
const bool bFsr = Settings.AntiAliasing == EBallsAntiAliasing::FSR && Fsr;
int32 Method = 0;
// Maps anti-aliasing methods: None (0), FXAA (1), TAA (2), TSR (4), and FSR (2 or 4).
switch (Settings.AntiAliasing) {
case EBallsAntiAliasing::None: Method = 0; break;
case EBallsAntiAliasing::FXAA: Method = 1; break;
case EBallsAntiAliasing::TAA:
case EBallsAntiAliasing::TAAU: Method = 2; break;
case EBallsAntiAliasing::TSR: Method = 4; break;
// FSR replaces the temporal upscaler, so it sits on TAA; without the plugin TSR is the closest match.
case EBallsAntiAliasing::FSR: Method = bFsr ? 2 : 4; break;
default: break;
}
Run(FString::Printf(TEXT("r.AntiAliasingMethod %d"), Method));

BallRenderSystem: streaming balls with indirect draws in Unity

BallRenderSystem unpacks native ball words into GPU structured buffers for indirect mesh draws.

Source: source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/Rendering/BallRenderSystem.cs

source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/Rendering/BallRenderSystem.cs lines 88-111
// Unpacked balls fill scratch NativeArray<QuadInstance>, uploaded to GPU for Graphics.RenderMeshIndirect.
int count = 0;
for (int index = 0; index < batch.count && count < capacity; ++index)
{
    uint word = packed[index];
    // Commits 69da882a1, 5767a6f2b: empty slots (0xFFFFFFFF) decoded as color 15 magenta dots.
    // An empty visibility slot is all ones: colour 15 at the far corner, drawn as a stray magenta ball.
    if ((word >> 28) >= BallPalette.ColorCount)
    {
        continue;
    }
    float x = (word & 0x3FFFu) * scaleX;
    float y = ((word >> 14) & 0x3FFFu) * scaleY;
    if (!float.IsFinite(x) || !float.IsFinite(y) || x < -1f || y < -1f ||
        x > board.columns + 1f || y > board.rows + 1f)
    {
        continue;
    }
    Color tint = BallPalette.For((int)(word >> 28));
    scratch[count++] = new QuadInstance
    {
        center = new Unity.Mathematics.float2(x, y),
        size = new Unity.Mathematics.float2(BallRadius, BallRadius),
        color = new Vector4(tint.r, tint.g, tint.b, 1f),
    };
}

The Burst physics backend and how it mirrors the C++ physics

Unity's Burst physics backend mirrors the C++ simulation kernels, providing an independent validation oracle.

Source: source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/Physics/Burst/BurstBallStepJob.cs, source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/Physics/Burst/BallStep.cs, data/docs/codebase.html

source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/Physics/Burst/BurstBallStepJob.cs lines 53-86
// BurstBallStore uses 24-byte cache-aligned structs; BurstBoard mirrors BrickBoard.
// Fixed 120 Hz accumulator and hit resolution mirror C++ simulation kernels.
public void Execute(int index)
{
    BallData ball = balls[index];

    // Substepping logic mirrors ScalarBackend.cpp and BallStepCore.h:254-263.
    // Step ball with substep support mirroring ScalarBackend.cpp and BallStepCore.h:254-263
    int hitBrick = BallStep.stepBallSubsteps(
        ref ball.x,
        ref ball.y,
        ref ball.vx,
        ref ball.vy,
        ball.color,
        ref ball.bounces,
        board,
        stepParams);

    balls[index] = ball;

    // Optional direct render packing
    if (packedBalls.IsCreated && index < packedBalls.Length)
    {
        packedBalls[index] = BallStep.packBallForRender(
            ball.x,
            ball.y,
            ball.color,
            stepParams.packScaleX,
            stepParams.packScaleY);
    }

    // Report brick hit if any occurred during substeps
    if (hitBrick != BallStep.noBrickHit)
    {
        hits.Enqueue(new BallHit((uint)index, hitBrick));
    }
}

ParityProbe: bit-for-bit parity between C++ and Burst C#

ParityProbe runs identical seeds across C++ and Burst to detect math divergence via 14-bit deltas.

Source: source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/App/ParityProbe.cs

source/frontends/unity/Assets/Balls/Runtime/App/ParityProbe.cs lines 18-29
// Injects identical seeds into C++ Scalar and C# Burst backends simultaneously.
// 14-bit packed coordinates turn drift into exact integer deltas; > 16 units flags divergence.
public enum ControlledMode
{
    Random200,      // Standard parity probe: 200 seeded balls, 300 ticks
    SimpleMotion,   // 1 ball in empty space, 60 ticks (pure motion, no bounces)
    WallBounce,     // 1 ball hitting wall, 60 ticks, 1 bounce (bounces < 3, no retarget)
    WallRetarget,   // 1 ball bouncing 3+ times, 200 ticks, triggering retargeting
    BrickBounce,    // 1 ball deflected by non-matching brick, 60 ticks
    BrickHit,       // 1 ball destroying matching brick and dying, 60 ticks
    CornerExact,    // 1 ball hitting exact corner seam between cells (3.0, 5.0), 60 ticks
    CornerNear,     // 1 ball hitting near corner (3.002, 5.0), 60 ticks
    CornerEmbedded, // 1 ball embedded at corner diagonal tie inside cell (3, 4), 60 ticks
}

// IL2CPP turns C# P/Invoke calls into direct C function pointers, eliminating marshaling thunks.
// Windows Editor retains Mono for rapid iteration and debugging.
// The client machines are Linux, so the player is IL2CPP there; Windows keeps Mono for fast iteration.
// Standalone Windows and Linux share one backend setting, so it is put back afterwards: otherwise every
// Windows build would leave ProjectSettings.asset modified.
var namedTarget = NamedBuildTarget.FromBuildTargetGroup(BuildPipeline.GetBuildTargetGroup(target));
ScriptingImplementation savedBackend = PlayerSettings.GetScriptingBackend(namedTarget);
// Enables IL2CPP for StandaloneLinux64 while keeping Mono2x for Windows.

    target == BuildTarget.StandaloneLinux64 ? ScriptingImplementation.IL2CPP : ScriptingImplementation.Mono2x);

Cross-engine trade-offs, duplication, and architectural improvements

Code shared across all front ends and what is duplicated

The C++ core isolates simulation and state, requiring front ends to implement only windowing, input, and drawing.

Source: data/docs/codebase.html

source/core/game/SessionHost.h lines 15-57
// Core (100% shared): GameSession, Simulation, threading, level parsing, ScreenFlow, and control routes.
// Front ends duplicate only window setup, input forwarding, and three draw primitives (rects, cells, balls).
// One game session that a UI drives the same way on either thread: it posts commands, ticks once per frame and reads
// the latest SessionView. SDL uses SessionRunner directly; this lets a front end keep both, to compare them or fall
// back. On the UI thread the view is rebuilt after each tick, without the balls: the UI draws them straight from the
// backend through liveSession().
// Wraps session execution across either single-threaded UI or background worker thread.
class SessionHost {
public:
    SessionHost(GameMode mode, LevelData level, BackendKind backend, SessionThreading threading);
    // Reads the files as well: on the physics thread off the UI's frame, reporting to `progress`; on the UI thread
    // at once. takeLoadOutcome() says how it went, and a host whose level did not load must not be used further.
    SessionHost(GameMode mode, LevelFiles files, BackendKind backend, SessionThreading threading,
                std::shared_ptr<LoadProgress> progress);
    SessionHost(const SessionHost&) = delete;
    SessionHost& operator=(const SessionHost&) = delete;

    bool isThreaded() const { return runner != nullptr; }
    // On the UI thread the command runs at once; on the physics thread at the start of its next frame.
    void post(SessionRunner::Command command);
    // GameSession::setBallCollisions; anything other than None comes back as a BallCollisionsChanged event.
    void setBallCollisions(bool enabled);
    // Once per UI frame. On the UI thread it steps the session by deltaSeconds unless paused, using the input's frame
    // times for the addition governor; on the physics thread it hands the input over and takes the newest view.
    void tick(const PhysicsFrameInput& input, float deltaSeconds);
    // Null only while the physics thread has not published its first view.
    std::shared_ptr<const SessionView> view() const { return latest; }
    // Backend switches and ball collisions changes since the last call, in order.
    std::vector<SessionEvent> takeEvents();
    // Once, after a host given LevelFiles has read them.
    std::optional<LevelLoadOutcome> takeLoadOutcome();
    // The session itself while it runs on the UI thread, for drawing straight from its backend; null otherwise.
    GameSession* liveSession() { return session.get(); }

private:
    void refreshView();

    std::unique_ptr<GameSession> session;
    std::unique_ptr<SessionRunner> runner;
    std::shared_ptr<const SessionView> latest;
    BoardSnapshots boards;
    std::vector<SessionEvent> events;
    std::optional<LevelLoadOutcome> loadOutcome;
    // On the game thread a physics frame is a tick that stepped the session.
    PhysicsRate rate;
};

        // Binds abstract FlowAction directly to native Slate SButton click delegate.

Front-end bugs, edge cases and what to improve next

Cross-engine development revealed edge cases in slot decoding and menu rebuilding, pointing toward GPU-to-GPU sharing.

Source: Commits 69da882a1, 5767a6f2b, 72f0c6081, 2fd7d321a

source/frontends/unreal/Source/Balls/Private/UI/SBallsMenus.cpp lines 747-756
// Commits 69da882a1, 5767a6f2b: empty slots (0xFFFFFFFF) decoded as color 15 magenta corner dots.
// Commit 2fd7d321a preserved _main; commit 204b2e321 set EV 13 exposure to match SDL.
// Future work: GPU-to-GPU sharing via vkGetMemoryWin32HandleKHR between Vulkan physics and RDG/Unity.
EActiveTimerReturnType SBallsLevelSelect::Poll(double, float) {
    std::string Rows;
    for (const FlowLevelRow& Row : App->GetScreenFlow().levelRows()) {
        Rows += Row.name + (Row.highlighted ? "*\n" : "\n");
    }
    // Commit 72f0c6081: fixed menu flickering and lost hover by rebuilding Slate rows only on change.
    if (Rows != BuiltRows) {
        RebuildList();
    }
    return EActiveTimerReturnType::Continue;
}

Key facts

  • Ball data is packed into a single 32-bit unsigned integer: bits 0-13 for X (0-16383), bits 14-27 for Y (0-16383), and bits 28-30 for color palette index (0-6).
  • Empty visibility slots are encoded as 0xFFFFFFFFu; drawing code must filter (word >> 28) >= 7 to avoid stray magenta corner balls.
  • Halving visibility grid slots to 1.0 ball radius reduced background show-through from 16.6% to 0.1% for only 0.21-0.34 ms GPU time on a GTX 1650 (commit c4e70dae9).
  • Dense boards below 4 pixels per cell switch from instanced geometry to a single-pass cell image quad (CellImage.hlsl / BallsCells.usf), boosting 2048x2048 FPS from 9 to 1,297 in SDL and 4.3 to 419 in Unreal.
  • NVRHI Vulkan is the default desktop renderer everywhere—including Windows—so rendering and Vulkan physics compute share a single driver stack.
  • Off-thread renderer preparation in SDL (prepareRenderer) reduced GPU switching hitches from 345-660 ms down to a single frame (commit 3b523ecff).
  • The browser build statically links CPU physics, mounts assets at /assets via MEMFS, and runs single-threaded to avoid cross-origin isolation requirements on static hosts.
  • WebGPU browser async callbacks (adapters, devices, buffer mapping) are bridged to synchronous C++ via Emscripten's ASYNCIFY (emscripten_sleep(16)).
  • Unreal compiles the core externally via CMake (BallsCore, ModuleType.External), ensuring identical C++20 compiler flags and warning behavior across all front ends.
  • Unreal renders balls after motion blur in HDR scene color using an RDG scene view extension and equilateral circumscribing triangles (3 vertices, QuadCorners[3] in BallsDraw.usf).
  • Unity communicates with the core across a flat 169-function C ABI (source/plugin/BallsUnityApi.h, version 7) using blittable structs and opaque IntPtr handles.
  • Unity's Burst backend mirrors C++ physics kernels, validated tick-for-tick by ParityProbe across controlled motion, bounce, and corner scenarios.
  • Standalone Linux Unity builds target IL2CPP, converting C# to native C++ to eliminate Mono JIT marshaling thunks and match desktop execution speed.
